mir_eval
========

Python library for computing common heuristic accuracy scores for various music/audio information retrieval/signal processing tasks.

Documentation, including installation and usage information: https://mir-evaluation.github.io/mir_eval/

Dependencies:

* `Scipy/Numpy <http://www.scipy.org/>`_
* `decorator <https://github.com/micheles/decorator>`_

If you use mir_eval in a research project, please cite the following paper:

Colin Raffel, Brian McFee, Eric J. Humphrey, Justin Salamon, Oriol Nieto, Dawen Liang, and Daniel P. W. Ellis, "`mir_eval: A Transparent Implementation of Common MIR Metrics <http://colinraffel.com/publications/ismir2014mir_eval.pdf>`_", Proceedings of the 15th International Conference on Music Information Retrieval, 2014.




===== https://mir-eval.readthedocs.io/stable/api/alignment.html#mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error =====
mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error(reference_timestamps, estimated_timestamps)

Compute the absolute deviations between estimated and reference timestamps, and then returns the median and average over all events

Parameters:

reference_timestampsnp.ndarray

reference timestamps, in seconds

estimated_timestampsnp.ndarray

estimated timestamps, in seconds

Returns:

maefloat

Median absolute error

aae: float

Average absolute error

Examples

>>> reference_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('reference.txt')
>>> estimated_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('estimated.txt')
>>> mae, aae = mir_eval.align.absolute_error(reference_onsets, estimated_timestamps)

Documentation:
## API Test: `python:mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error`

### Signature
```python
def absolute_error(reference_timestamps, estimated_timestamps)
```
_Source: source/mir_eval/alignment.py:115_

_Source doc:_ Compute the absolute deviations between estimated and reference timestamps,
and then returns the median and average over all events

Examples
--------
>>> reference_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('reference.txt')
>>> estimated_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('estimated.txt')
>>> mae, aae = mir_eval.align.absolute_error(reference_onsets, estimated_timestamps)

Parameters
----------
reference_timestamps : np.ndarray
    reference timestamps, in seconds
estimated_timestamps : np.ndarray
    estimated timestamps, in seconds

Returns
-------
mae : float
    Median absolute error
aae: float
    Average absolute error

### Goal
Computes the median and average absolute deviation between a set of estimated timestamps and ground-truth reference timestamps to evaluate alignment accuracy.

### Parameters
- `reference_timestamps`: A 1D `np.ndarray` containing ground-truth event timestamps in seconds.
- `estimated_timestamps`: A 1D `np.ndarray` containing the estimated event timestamps in seconds.

### Input
- **Data Format:** Both inputs must be 1D `np.ndarray` objects representing time in seconds.
- **Preconditions:** Inputs should be sorted by time. The arrays must contain the same number of events to compute a direct correspondence.

### Output
Returns a tuple `(mae, aae)`:
- `mae` (float): The median of the absolute differences between the reference and estimated timestamps.
- `aae` (float): The arithmetic mean of the absolute differences between the reference and estimated timestamps.

### Valid Call Patterns
```python
import mir_eval
# Assuming reference_timestamps and estimated_timestamps are loaded via mir_eval.io.load_events
mae, aae = mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error(reference_timestamps, estimated_timestamps)
```

### LLM Instruction Prompt
- When calling `mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error`, ensure both `reference_timestamps` and `estimated_timestamps` are 1D NumPy arrays of floats representing time in seconds.
- Ensure the arrays are sorted chronologically before passing them to the function.
- Note that the function expects a direct mapping between the two arrays; ensure the number of elements matches.

### Prompt Snippet
```text
# Example usage of absolute_error for alignment evaluation
mae, aae = mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error(ref_times, est_times)
```

### Common Failure Modes
- **Shape Mismatch:** Passing arrays of different lengths will result in a `ValueError` during the subtraction operation.
- **Unsorted Input:** If the input arrays are not sorted by time, the computed errors will not reflect the intended temporal alignment.
- **Unit Mismatch:** Providing timestamps in samples rather than seconds will lead to incorrect metric values.

### Fix Code Hint
```python
import numpy as np
# Ensure inputs are sorted and of equal length
ref_sorted = np.sort(reference_timestamps)
est_sorted = np.sort(estimated_timestamps)
if len(ref_sorted) == len(est_sorted):
    mae, aae = mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error(ref_sorted, est_sorted)
```

Project context distilled from the original README (grounding — may say none exists):
===== Original documentation part 1/2 =====
### 1. Project purpose
`mir_eval` is a scientific Python library designed for the reproducible evaluation of Music Information Retrieval (MIR) systems. It provides standardized implementations of metrics for common MIR tasks, I/O utilities for loading standard annotation formats, and visualization/sonification tools to assist in qualitative analysis.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Annotation Loading:** Use `mir_eval.io` to load ground-truth and estimated data from text files into NumPy arrays.
*   **Metric Computation:** Pass loaded arrays to task-specific modules (e.g., `mir_eval.beat`, `mir_eval.chord`, `mir_eval.melody`) to compute performance scores.
*   **Evaluation Aggregation:** Use the `evaluate()` function provided in most modules to compute a comprehensive suite of metrics for a given task.
*   **Visualization:** Use `mir_eval.display` to generate plots (e.g., piano rolls, pitch contours, segmentations) using Matplotlib.
*   **Sonification:** Use `mir_eval.sonify` to create audio representations of annotations for "evaluation by ear."

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Task Modules:** Each task (e.g., `beat`, `chord`, `melody`, `onset`, `segment`, `hierarchy`, `multipitch`, `pattern`, `alignment`, `key`, `separation`) contains a `validate()` function to check input formats and an `evaluate()` function for batch metric calculation.
*   **Data Structures:**
    *   **Events:** 1D NumPy arrays of timestamps (seconds).
    *   **Intervals:** $N \times 2$ NumPy arrays (start, end).
    *   **Labeled Intervals:** $N \times 2$ intervals + list of $N$ labels.
    *   **Ragged Time Series:** Used for multi-pitch/multi-source data where the number of values per timestamp varies.
*   **Chord Encoding:** The `mir_eval.chord` module provides complex utilities to parse, encode, and compare chord labels using sub-alphabets (e.g., `majmin`, `triads`, `sevenths`).

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Delimited Text:** `mir_eval.io.load_delimited` is the base loader.
*   **Specific Loaders:**
    *   `load_events`: Single column of floats.
    *   `load_labeled_events`: Time (float) + Label (str).
    *   `load_intervals`: Start (float) + End (float).
    *   `load_labeled_intervals`: Start (float) + End (float) + Label (str).
    *   `load_ragged_time_series`: Time (float) + variable number of values.
    *   `load_patterns`: MIREX 2013 format (list of lists of tuples).

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Metric Scores:** Most `evaluate()` functions return a dictionary mapping metric names (strings) to scores (floats).
*   **Visualizations:** Matplotlib `Axes` objects.
*   **Audio:** NumPy arrays representing synthesized signals (e.g., click tracks, piano rolls).

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Dependencies:** Requires `numpy`, `scipy`, and `matplotlib`.
*   **Conventions:** Time is always in seconds. Frequencies are in Hz (or cents for melody). Chord labels follow standard syntax (e.g., `C:maj`).

### 7. Commands and examples

**Beat Evaluation:**
```python
>>> reference_beats = mir_eval.io.load_events('reference.txt')
>>> reference_beats = mir_eval.beat.trim_beats(reference_beats)
>>> estimated_beats = mir_eval.io.load_events('estimated.txt')
>>> estimated_beats = mir_eval.beat.trim_beats(estimated_beats)
>>> scores = mir_eval.beat.evaluate(reference_beats, estimated_beats)
```

**Chord Evaluation:**
```python
>>> (ref_intervals, ref_labels) = mir_eval.io.load_labeled_intervals('ref.lab')
>>> (est_intervals, est_labels) = mir_eval.io.load_labeled_intervals('est.lab')
>>> scores = mir_eval.chord.evaluate(ref_intervals, ref_labels,
...                                  est_intervals, est_labels)
```

**Melody Evaluation:**
```python
>>> ref_time, ref_freq = mir_eval.io.load_time_series('ref.txt')
>>> est_time, est_freq = mir_eval.io.load_time_series('est.txt')
>>> scores = mir_eval.melody.evaluate(ref_time, ref_freq,
...                                   est_time, est_freq)
```

**Segmentation Evaluation:**
```python
>>> (ref_intervals, ref_labels) = mir_eval.io.load_labeled_intervals('ref.lab')
>>> (est_intervals, est_labels) = mir_eval.io.load_labeled_intervals('est.lab')
>>> scores = mir_eval.segment.evaluate(ref_intervals, ref_labels,
...                                    est_intervals, est_labels)
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules
*   **Preconditions:** For most metrics, reference and estimated arrays must be sorted by time.
*   **Interval Adjustment:** Use `mir_eval.util.adjust_intervals` to ensure estimated intervals span the same duration as reference intervals before evaluation.
*   **Deprecation:** `mir_eval.io.load_wav` is deprecated; use `soundfile` instead. `mir_eval.separation` is deprecated; use `sigsep-museval`.
*   **Chord Labels:** Invalid labels raise `InvalidChordException`.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   `mir_eval` is the standard for reproducible MIR research.
*   Metrics are based on established literature (e.g., MIREX, BSS_EVAL).
*   The library supports both flat and hierarchical structure evaluation.
*   All time-based inputs must be in seconds.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   The `mir_eval.sonify.pitch_contour` documentation is truncated in the provided source.
*   The `mir_eval.display` module lacks comprehensive examples for all plot types.
*   The distinction between "transitive" and "reduced" modes in `hierarchy` metrics could be explained with more theoretical depth.

===== Original documentation part 2/2 =====
### 1. Project purpose
`mir_eval` is a scientific Python library designed to provide transparent, reproducible, and standardized implementations of common evaluation metrics for Music Information Retrieval (MIR) and audio signal processing tasks. It serves as a centralized repository for metrics used in research, ensuring that evaluation procedures (such as those used in MIREX) are consistent across different studies.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Metric Evaluation:** Comparing reference (ground truth) annotations against estimated outputs using task-specific modules (e.g., `tempo`, `transcription`, `transcription_velocity`).
*   **Data Preprocessing:** Normalizing, validating, and transforming annotation formats (e.g., converting intervals to samples, adjusting event times to specific ranges, or converting frequency to MIDI).
*   **Sonification:** Synthesizing audio from symbolic or feature-based representations (e.g., pitch contours, chromagrams, or chord labels) for verification or debugging.
*   **Matching:** Computing optimal correspondences between reference and estimated events (onsets, offsets, notes) using bipartite graph matching.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Task Modules:** Each task (e.g., `mir_eval.tempo`, `mir_eval.transcription`) provides an `evaluate()` function that acts as a high-level entry point, returning a dictionary of all relevant metrics.
*   **Validation:** Every module includes `validate()` functions to ensure input arrays (intervals, pitches, velocities) conform to expected shapes and constraints before computation.
*   **Matching Logic:** The `transcription` and `transcription_velocity` modules use `match_notes()` to establish correspondences based on onset, offset, pitch, and (optionally) velocity tolerances before calculating precision/recall.
*   **Utility Functions:** The `mir_eval.util` module provides cross-task helpers for label indexing, interval manipulation, and mathematical conversions (e.g., `hz_to_midi`).

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Intervals:** `np.ndarray` of shape `(n, 2)` representing `[onset, offset]` pairs.
*   **Pitches:** `np.ndarray` of shape `(n,)` representing fundamental frequency (F0) in Hertz.
*   **Velocities:** `np.ndarray` of shape `(n,)` representing MIDI velocities in the range `[0, 127]`.
*   **Tempi:** `np.ndarray` of length 2 (ascending order) representing BPM.
*   **Chromagrams:** `np.ndarray` of shape `(12, n_times)`.

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Metrics:** Scalar values (floats) for Precision, Recall, F-measure, and Average Overlap Ratio.
*   **Dictionaries:** The `evaluate()` functions return a `dict` mapping metric names to their computed values.
*   **Synthesized Audio:** `np.ndarray` representing time-domain audio signals generated from symbolic inputs.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Dependencies:** Requires `numpy`, `scipy`, and `decorator`.
*   **Headless Operation:** The library is designed for non-interactive, headless evaluation environments.
*   **Deterministic Matching:** Uses bipartite graph matching for note-tracking, which differs from the greedy matching used in some legacy MIREX scripts.

### 7. Commands and examples

**Transcription Evaluation:**
```python
ref_intervals, ref_pitches = mir_eval.io.load_valued_intervals('reference.txt')
est_intervals, est_pitches = mir_eval.io.load_valued_intervals('estimated.txt')
scores = mir_eval.transcription.evaluate(ref_intervals, ref_pitches,
    est_intervals, est_pitches)
```

**Tempo Detection:**
```python
# Example of computing tempo detection accuracy
p_score, one_correct, both_correct = mir_eval.tempo.detection(
    reference_tempi, reference_weight, estimated_tempi)
```

**File Intersection Utility:**
```python
flist1 = ['/a/b/abc.lab', '/c/d/123.lab', '/e/f/xyz.lab']
flist2 = ['/g/h/xyz.npy', '/i/j/123.txt', '/k/l/456.lab']
sublist1, sublist2 = mir_eval.util.intersect_files(flist1, flist2)
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
*   **Transcription Matching:** `match_notes()` requires `ref_intervals` and `est_intervals` to be validated. The `strict` parameter toggles between `<` (strict) and `<=` (default) for threshold comparisons.
*   **Velocity Normalization:** In `transcription_velocity`, reference velocities are rescaled to `[0, 1]`. Estimated velocities are rescaled via linear regression to minimize L2 distance to reference velocities before thresholding.
*   **Tempo:** Reference tempi must be positive and provided in ascending order. `reference_weight` must be a float in `[0, 1]`.
*   **Intervals:** Intervals are assumed to be disjoint in many utility functions. `intervals_to_samples` rounds intervals down to the nearest multiple of `sample_size`.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   **Citation:** Users must cite: Raffel, C., McFee, B., Humphrey, E. J., Salamon, J., Nieto, O., Liang, D., & Ellis, D. P. W. (2014). "mir_eval: A Transparent Implementation of Common MIR Metrics".
*   **MIREX Differences:** Note that `mir_eval` uses bipartite matching and 4-decimal point rounding, which may result in slightly different scores compared to legacy MIREX scripts.
*   **Instrument Agnostic:** The transcription module is instrument-agnostic; all estimated notes are compared against all reference notes.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   The documentation for `mir_eval.io` is referenced in docstrings (e.g., `load_valued_intervals`) but is not explicitly detailed in the provided text.
*   Specific behavior for empty input arrays in `evaluate()` functions is not explicitly defined, though `validate()` functions are present.
*   The `sonify` module parameters (e.g., `function=<ufunc 'sin'>`) lack detailed examples of custom synthesis functions.

mir_eval
========

Python library for computing common heuristic accuracy scores for various music/audio information retrieval/signal processing tasks.

Documentation, including installation and usage information: https://mir-evaluation.github.io/mir_eval/

Dependencies:

* `Scipy/Numpy <http://www.scipy.org/>`_
* `decorator <https://github.com/micheles/decorator>`_

If you use mir_eval in a research project, please cite the following paper:

Colin Raffel, Brian McFee, Eric J. Humphrey, Justin Salamon, Oriol Nieto, Dawen Liang, and Daniel P. W. Ellis, "`mir_eval: A Transparent Implementation of Common MIR Metrics <http://colinraffel.com/publications/ismir2014mir_eval.pdf>`_", Proceedings of the 15th International Conference on Music Information Retrieval, 2014.

mir_eval.alignment.absolute_error(reference_timestamps, estimated_timestamps)

Compute the absolute deviations between estimated and reference timestamps, and then returns the median and average over all events

Parameters:

reference_timestampsnp.ndarray

reference timestamps, in seconds

estimated_timestampsnp.ndarray

estimated timestamps, in seconds

Returns:

maefloat

Median absolute error

aae: float

Average absolute error

Examples

>>> reference_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('reference.txt')
>>> estimated_timestamps = mir_eval.io.load_events('estimated.txt')
>>> mae, aae = mir_eval.align.absolute_error(reference_onsets, estimated_timestamps)

===== Shared original document: README.md =====
_*October 1, 2025: Thumbnailator 0.4.21 has been released!
See [Changes](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Changes) for details.*_

_*Thumbnailator is now available through
[Maven](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Maven)!*_

# What is Thumbnailator?

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/coobird/thumbnailator/img/home/home-image.png)

_Thumbnailator_ is a thumbnail generation library for Java.

# Why Thumbnailator?
Making high-quality thumbnails in Java can be a fairly difficult task.

Learning how to use the Image I/O API, Java 2D API, image processing,
image scaling techniques, ... but fear not! _Thumbnailator_ will take care
of all those things for you!

Thumbnailator is a single JAR file with no dependencies to external libraries,
making development and deployment simple and easy. It is also available on
the Maven Central Repository for easy inclusion in Maven projects.

# How simple is Thumbnailator?

_Thumbnailator_'s fluent interface can be used to perform fairly complicated
thumbnail processing task in one simple step.

For example, creating JPEG thumbnails of image files in a directory, all
resized to a maximum dimension of 640 pixels by 480 pixels while preserving
the aspect ratio of the original image can be performed by the following:

```
Thumbnails.of(new File("path/to/directory").listFiles())
    .size(640, 480)
    .outputFormat("jpg")
    .toFiles(Rename.PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL);
```

The fluent interface provided by the _Thumbnailator_ simplifies the task of
making thumbnails into a single method call!

No need to access the Image I/O API and manually manipulate `BufferedImage`s
through `Graphics2D` objects. _Thumbnailator_ does all of that for you.

# What can Thumbnailator do?

The following pages have more information on what _Thumbnailator_ can do:

* [Features](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Features)
* [Examples](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Examples)
* [Thumbnailator API Documentation](https://coobird.github.io/thumbnailator/javadoc/0.4.21/)
* [Frequently Asked Questions](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/FAQ)

# Disclaimer
*Thumbnailator is still early in its development, and the APIs are subject to
change at any time.*

# License
Thumbnailtor is released under the MIT License.

## API Test: `java:net.coobird.thumbnailator.Thumbnails.Builder.asBufferedImage`

### Signature
```java
():BufferedImage
```
_Source: source/src/main/java/net/coobird/thumbnailator/Thumbnails.java:2342_

### Goal
Generates a thumbnail from a single source image and returns the result as an in-memory `BufferedImage` object.

### Parameters
_None._

### Input
The caller must provide a single source image (e.g., `File`, `InputStream`, `URL`, or `BufferedImage`) via the `Thumbnails.of()` or `Thumbnails.from...()` entry points. The builder must be configured with a valid transformation (e.g., `.size()`, `.scale()`) before calling this method.

### Output
Returns `BufferedImage` — The processed thumbnail image. The image type typically matches the input image type unless explicitly overridden via `imageType(int)`.

### Valid Call Patterns
```java
BufferedImage thumbnail = Thumbnails.of("image.jpg")
    .size(200, 200)
    .asBufferedImage();
```

### LLM Instruction Prompt
- Use `asBufferedImage()` only when the builder is configured with a single source image.
- Ensure the builder has a defined transformation (size or scale) before invocation.
- Do not use this method if the intent is to write the result directly to a file; use `toFile()` instead to avoid potential color issues with the default JPEG writer when handling alpha channels.

### Prompt Snippet
```text
// Example of generating a thumbnail in-memory
BufferedImage result = Thumbnails.of(sourceFile)
    .size(100, 100)
    .asBufferedImage();
```

### Common Failure Modes
- **`IllegalArgumentException`**: Thrown if the builder was initialized with multiple source images (e.g., a directory of files).
- **`IOException`**: Thrown if an error occurs during the reading of the original image.
- **`IllegalStateException`**: Thrown if both `size()` and `scale()` are called on the same builder instance.
- **Color/Alpha Issues**: Using the returned `BufferedImage` with the default Java JPEG writer may result in incorrect colors or exceptions if the image contains an alpha channel.

### Fix Code Hint
```java
// If writing to a file, prefer toFile() to handle color/alpha workarounds
Thumbnails.of("input.png")
    .size(100, 100)
    .toFile("output.png");
```

- for writers, read the output back and compare representative content or a known
  aggregate with the input.

Project context distilled from the original README (grounding — may say none exists):
===== Original documentation part 1/2 =====
### 1. Project purpose
Thumbnailator is a Java library designed to simplify high-quality thumbnail generation. It abstracts the complexities of the Java Image I/O and Java 2D APIs, providing a fluent interface for common image processing tasks such as resizing, cropping, rotating, and watermarking. It is a single-JAR library with no external dependencies, suitable for headless environments.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Fluent Pipeline:** The primary entry point is the `Thumbnails` class, which provides a builder-style fluent interface to define source, transformation, and destination parameters in a single chain.
*   **Task-Based Processing:** For more granular control, developers can use `ThumbnailTask` implementations (e.g., `FileThumbnailTask`, `StreamThumbnailTask`, `SourceSinkThumbnailTask`) to manage specific I/O operations.
*   **Filtering:** Image transformations are handled by classes implementing the `ImageFilter` interface, which can be chained using the `Pipeline` class.
*   **Exif Handling:** The library includes utilities (`ExifUtils`, `ExifFilterUtils`) to detect and apply orientation corrections based on Exif metadata.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Entry Point:** `net.coobird.thumbnailator.Thumbnails` is the primary class for initiating thumbnail generation.
*   **Builders:** `Thumbnails.Builder<T>` manages the configuration of the generation task.
*   **Makers:** `FixedSizeThumbnailMaker` and `ScaledThumbnailMaker` handle the core resizing logic.
*   **Geometry:** `Position` (and the `Positions` enum) and `Size` (and its implementations `AbsoluteSize`, `RelativeSize`) define where and how elements like watermarks or captions are placed.
*   **Naming:** The `Rename` class provides standard strategies for generating output filenames (e.g., `PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL`).
*   **Filters:** `ImageFilter` implementations include `Watermark`, `Caption`, `Canvas`, `Rotation`, `Flip`, `Colorize`, and `Transparency`.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
Thumbnailator supports various input sources:
*   **Files:** `File` objects via `FileImageSource`.
*   **Streams:** `InputStream` via `InputStreamImageSource`.
*   **URLs:** `URL` via `URLImageSource`.
*   **In-memory:** `BufferedImage` via `BufferedImageSource`.
*   **Format Support:** Relies on standard Java Image I/O; supported formats can be queried via `ThumbnailatorUtils.getSupportedOutputFormats()`.

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Files:** `FileImageSink` writes to the filesystem.
*   **Streams:** `OutputStreamImageSink` writes to an `OutputStream`.
*   **In-memory:** `BufferedImageSink` returns a `BufferedImage`.
*   **Naming:** `ConsecutivelyNumberedFilenames` can be used to generate sequential filenames for batch operations.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Headless:** Designed to run in headless environments; avoids UI components.
*   **System Properties:** Configuration properties (e.g., `thumbnailator.disableExifWorkaround`, `thumbnailator.debugLog`) can be set via JVM `-D` flags or a `thumbnailator.properties` file on the classpath.
*   **Memory:** A `CONSERVE_MEMORY_WORKAROUND` is available for large images, though it is not enabled by default.

### 7. Commands and examples

**Fluent API Usage:**
```java
Thumbnails.of(new File("path/to/directory").listFiles())
    .size(640, 480)
    .outputFormat("jpg")
    .toFiles(Rename.PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL);
```

**Fixed Size Maker:**
```java
BufferedImage img = ImageIO.read(new File("sourceImage.jpg"));
BufferedImage thumbnail = new FixedSizeThumbnailMaker()
    .size(200, 200)
    .keepAspectRatio(true)
    .make(img);
```

**Scaled Maker:**
```java
BufferedImage img = ImageIO.read(new File("sourceImage.jpg"));
BufferedImage thumbnail = new ScaledThumbnailMaker()
    .scale(0.25)
    .make(img);
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
*   **Preconditions:** For `FixedSizeThumbnailMaker`, the size must be set before calling `make()`.
*   **Constraints:** `ThumbnailTask` implementations only process the first image in a multi-image file; subsequent images are ignored.
*   **Type Selection:** `IfdType` and `Orientation` enums are used for Exif metadata parsing. `IfdType` defines data types (e.g., `BYTE`, `ASCII`, `SHORT`, `LONG`, `RATIONAL`) as per Exif 2.3.
*   **Compatibility:** The `PREFIX_HYPTHEN_THUMBNAIL` and `SUFFIX_HYPTHEN_THUMBNAIL` constants in `Rename` are deprecated and will be removed in 0.5.0.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   Thumbnailator is a single-JAR library with no external dependencies.
*   The library is released under the MIT License.
*   The fluent interface is the recommended way to perform complex tasks in a single step.
*   The library provides specific workarounds for memory management and Exif metadata handling in JRE-bundled JPEG readers.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   The documentation does not explicitly detail the full list of supported image formats beyond stating it relies on the Java Image I/O API.
*   The `ThumbnailParameter` class is mentioned as a core component but lacks a detailed breakdown of all available parameters in the provided snippets.
*   The `Caption` class constructor details were truncated in the provided documentation.

===== Original documentation part 2/2 =====
### 1. Project purpose
Thumbnailator is a Java library designed for high-quality, deterministic image processing. It provides a fluent, readable API for generating thumbnails from various sources (files, streams, URLs, and `BufferedImage` objects). It supports resizing, cropping, rotation, watermarking, and applying custom image filters, while abstracting away the complexities of Java's `ImageIO` and `Graphics2D` APIs.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Fluent Pipeline:** The primary workflow involves starting with a `Thumbnails.of(...)` or `Thumbnails.from...(...)` entry point, chaining configuration methods (e.g., `.size()`, `.rotate()`, `.watermark()`), and terminating with an output method (e.g., `.toFile()`, `.asBufferedImage()`).
*   **Batch Processing:** The library supports processing collections of inputs (files, streams, etc.) and outputting them to directories or lists using `Rename` strategies.
*   **Custom Resizing:** Users can select specific resizing algorithms (e.g., `Bilinear`, `Bicubic`, `ProgressiveBilinear`) via the `Resizers` enum or `ResizerFactory` implementations.
*   **Filter Application:** Users can apply `ImageFilter` implementations (like `Caption`) to images after the resizing step.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **`Thumbnails.Builder<T>`:** The core fluent interface. It is recommended to chain methods directly from the `Thumbnails.of` call to the terminal output method without storing the builder in a variable.
*   **`ThumbnailParameter`:** Holds the configuration for a thumbnail task. It is mutable and should not be reused across multiple tasks.
*   **`Resizer` / `ResizerFactory`:** Interfaces for defining how images are scaled. `DefaultResizerFactory` is the standard implementation, selecting the optimal algorithm based on source/destination dimensions.
*   **`Rename`:** A functional interface used during batch file operations to determine output filenames (e.g., `Rename.PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL`).
*   **`BufferedImageBuilder`:** A utility for creating `BufferedImage` instances with specific dimensions and types.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Sources:** `File`, `String` (filenames), `URL`, `InputStream`, and `BufferedImage`.
*   **Formats:** Supports any format recognized by the Java Image I/O API. Use `ImageIO.getWriterFormatNames()` to check supported output formats.
*   **Metadata:** Supports using Exif metadata for orientation via `useExifOrientation(boolean)`.

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Destinations:** `File`, `OutputStream`, `BufferedImage`, or `List<BufferedImage>`.
*   **Quality:** Controlled via `outputQuality(double)` (0.0 to 1.0).
*   **Format:** Controlled via `outputFormat(String)`. `determineOutputFormat()` can infer the format from file extensions.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Headless:** Designed for headless environments; avoids UI components.
*   **Memory:** For large batches, `iterableBufferedImages()` is preferred over `asBufferedImages()` to avoid `OutOfMemoryError`.
*   **Thread Safety:** `ThumbnailParameter` is not thread-safe.
*   **Compatibility:** Designed for JDK 8+.

### 7. Commands and examples

**Basic Resize and Save:**
```java
Thumbnails.of("path/to/image")
    .size(200, 200)
    .toFile("path/to/thumbnail");
```

**Batch Processing with Rename:**
```java
Thumbnails.of(directory.listFiles())
    .size(200, 200)
    .outputFormat("jpeg")
    .asFiles(Rename.PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL);
```

**Using a Specific Resizer:**
```java
BufferedImage sourceImage = new BufferedImageBuilder(400, 400).build();
BufferedImage destImage = new BufferedImageBuilder(200, 200).build();
Resizers.BILINEAR.resize(sourceImage, destImage);
```

**Applying a Caption Filter:**
```java
// Note: Caption implements ImageFilter
Thumbnails.of("image.jpg")
    .size(200, 200)
    .addFilter(new Caption("My Caption", Font.decode("Arial"), Color.WHITE, Positions.BOTTOM_RIGHT, 10))
    .toFile("output.jpg");
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
*   **IllegalStateExceptions:** Calling `scale()` after `size()` (or vice versa) throws an `IllegalStateException`.
*   **Overwriting:** `allowOverwrite(boolean)` controls whether existing files are replaced.
*   **Image Types:** When writing to files, avoid `asBufferedImages()` if the image has an alpha channel, as the default JPEG writer may cause color issues; use `toFiles()` instead.
*   **Resizer Selection:** `DefaultResizerFactory` automatically chooses `ProgressiveBilinearResizer` for most downscaling, `BicubicResizer` for upscaling, and `NullResizer` for identical dimensions.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   Thumbnailator is a fluent, chainable API.
*   It is designed for headless operation.
*   It provides deterministic resizing via `Resizer` implementations.
*   It handles Exif orientation automatically if enabled.
*   It provides memory-efficient batch processing via `Iterable` interfaces.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   The `ThumbnailTask` class is mentioned in `Thumbnailator.createThumbnail(ThumbnailTask)`, but its specific implementation details and usage are not fully elaborated in the provided docs.
*   The exact behavior of `ImageIO` writer support for specific formats (like WebP or HEIF) is dependent on the underlying JRE/JDK environment, which is not explicitly detailed.

===== Shared original document: README.md =====
_*October 1, 2025: Thumbnailator 0.4.21 has been released!
See [Changes](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Changes) for details.*_

_*Thumbnailator is now available through
[Maven](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Maven)!*_

# What is Thumbnailator?

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/coobird/thumbnailator/img/home/home-image.png)

_Thumbnailator_ is a thumbnail generation library for Java.

# Why Thumbnailator?
Making high-quality thumbnails in Java can be a fairly difficult task.

Learning how to use the Image I/O API, Java 2D API, image processing,
image scaling techniques, ... but fear not! _Thumbnailator_ will take care
of all those things for you!

Thumbnailator is a single JAR file with no dependencies to external libraries,
making development and deployment simple and easy. It is also available on
the Maven Central Repository for easy inclusion in Maven projects.

# How simple is Thumbnailator?

_Thumbnailator_'s fluent interface can be used to perform fairly complicated
thumbnail processing task in one simple step.

For example, creating JPEG thumbnails of image files in a directory, all
resized to a maximum dimension of 640 pixels by 480 pixels while preserving
the aspect ratio of the original image can be performed by the following:

```
Thumbnails.of(new File("path/to/directory").listFiles())
    .size(640, 480)
    .outputFormat("jpg")
    .toFiles(Rename.PREFIX_DOT_THUMBNAIL);
```

The fluent interface provided by the _Thumbnailator_ simplifies the task of
making thumbnails into a single method call!

No need to access the Image I/O API and manually manipulate `BufferedImage`s
through `Graphics2D` objects. _Thumbnailator_ does all of that for you.

# What can Thumbnailator do?

The following pages have more information on what _Thumbnailator_ can do:

* [Features](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Features)
* [Examples](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/Examples)
* [Thumbnailator API Documentation](https://coobird.github.io/thumbnailator/javadoc/0.4.21/)
* [Frequently Asked Questions](https://github.com/coobird/thumbnailator/wiki/FAQ)

# Disclaimer
*Thumbnailator is still early in its development, and the APIs are subject to
change at any time.*

# License
Thumbnailtor is released under the MIT License.

# SedonaDB

[![PyPI Version](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/sedonadb)](https://pypi.org/project/sedonadb/) [![PyPI Downloads](https://static.pepy.tech/personalized-badge/sedonadb?period=monthly&units=NONE&left_color=BLACK&right_color=GREEN&left_text=downloads%2Fmonth)](https://pepy.tech/projects/sedonadb) [![Downloads](https://static.pepy.tech/personalized-badge/sedonadb?period=total&units=international_system&left_color=black&right_color=brightgreen&left_text=total%20downloads)](https://pepy.tech/project/sedonadb)
[![Anaconda Version](
https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb/badges/version.svg)](https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb) [![Conda Downloads](https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb/badges/downloads.svg)](https://anaconda.org/channels/conda-forge/packages/sedonadb/overview)
[![R-multiverse
status](https://img.shields.io/badge/dynamic/json?url=https%3A%2F%2Fcommunity.r-multiverse.org%2Fapi%2Fpackages%2Fsedonadb&query=%24.Version&label=r-multiverse)](https://community.r-multiverse.org/sedonadb)

SedonaDB is an open-source single-node analytical database engine with **geospatial as a first-class citizen**. It aims to deliver the fastest spatial analytics query speed and the most comprehensive function coverage available.

SedonaDB is perfect for processing smaller to medium datasets on local machines or cloud instances. For distributed workloads, you can leverage the power of SedonaSpark, SedonaFlink, or SedonaSnow.

## Architecture

![SedonaDB Architecture](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sedonadb-architecture.svg)

* **Columnar in-memory datasets**
    * Spatial indexing
    * Spatial statistics
    * CRS tracking
    * Arrow format and zero serialization overhead

* **Spatial query optimization**
    * Spatial-aware heuristic based optimization
    * Spatial-aware cost based optimization
    * Automatic disk spilling for large-scale spatial joins

* **Spatial query processing**
    * Spatial range query, KNN query, spatial join query, KNN join query
    * Map algebra, NDVI, mask, zonal statistics

Raster functions are coming soon. We expect SedonaDB Raster will match all raster functions provided in [SedonaSpark](https://sedona.apache.org/latest/api/sql/Raster-operators/).

## Features of SedonaDB

SedonaDB has several advantages:

* **High Performance:** Built in Rust for exceptional speed and memory efficiency
* **Comprehensive Spatial Toolkit:** Supports both vector and raster functions in a single library
* **CRS Propagation:** Always maintains coordinate reference system information
* **Format Flexibility:** Supports legacy and modern file formats including GeoParquet, Shapefile, GeoJSON
* **Dual APIs:** Python and SQL interfaces for seamless workflow integration
* **Extensible:** Easily customizable and extensible architecture
* **Ecosystem Integration:** Interoperable with PyArrow-compatible libraries like GeoPandas, DuckDB, and Polars
* **Active Community:** Great maintainers and contributors who encourage external contributions

## Performance Benchmarks

This is a performance benchmark comparing SedonaDB 0.1.0, DuckDB 1.4.0, and GeoPandas 1.1.1 using SpatialBench Queries 1-12 at Scale Factors 1 and 10. Details can be found at [Apache Sedona SpatialBench](https://sedona.apache.org/spatialbench/single-node-benchmarks/).

  <img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sf1-09242025.png" alt="SF1 Benchmark Results" width="45%" />
  <img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sf10-09242025.png" alt="SF10 Benchmark Results" width="45%" />
</div>

## Install

You can install Python SedonaDB with [PyPI](https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/tutorials/installing-packages/):

```sh
pip install "apache-sedona[db]"
```

Or with [conda](https://www.anaconda.com/docs/getting-started/main):

```sh
conda install sedonadb
```

## Quick Start

Get started with SedonaDB in just a few lines:

```python
import sedona.db

# Connect to SedonaDB
sd = sedona.db.connect()

# Run a simple spatial query
result = sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(0, 1) as geom")
result.show()
```

### Supported File Formats

SedonaDB supports a wide range of geospatial file formats:

- **Vector:** GeoParquet, WKT, WKB, all formats supported by GeoPandas
- **Raster:** Coming soon with full SedonaSpark compatibility

## Overture buildings example

This section shows how to query the Overture buildings data.

Start by establishing a connection:

```python
import sedona.db

sd = sedona.db.connect()
```

Read the dataset into a Python SedonaDB `DataFrame`. This is lazy: even though the Overture buildings table contains millions of rows, SedonaDB will only fetch the data required for the query.

```python
df = sd.read_parquet(
    "s3://overturemaps-us-west-2/release/2026-02-18.0/theme=buildings/type=building/",
    options={"aws.skip_signature": True, "aws.region": "us-west-2"},
)
df.to_view("buildings")
```

Now run a query to compute the centroids of tall buildings (above 20 meters) in New York City:

```python
nyc_bbox_wkt = (
    "POLYGON((-74.2591 40.4774, -74.2591 40.9176, -73.7004 40.9176, -73.7004 40.4774, -74.2591 40.4774))"
)

sd.sql(f"""
SELECT
    id,
    height,
    num_floors,
    roof_shape,
    ST_Centroid(geometry) as centroid
FROM
    buildings
WHERE
    is_underground = FALSE
    AND height IS NOT NULL
    AND height > 20
    AND ST_Intersects(geometry, ST_GeomFromText('{nyc_bbox_wkt}', 4326))
LIMIT 5;
""").show()
```

title: ST_Point
description: Constructs a Point Geometry from X and Y.
kernels:
  - returns: geometry
    args:
    - name: x
      type: double
    - name: "y"
      type: double
    - name: srid
      type: crs
---

## Description

Constructs a Point geometry from X and Y coordinates. An optional SRID or CRS can be provided as a third argument.

## Examples

```sql
SELECT ST_AsText(ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128));
```

With an SRID:

```sql
SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 4326);
```

With a CRS:

```sql
SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 'EPSG:3857');
```


### Goal
Constructs a new `geometry` object of type Point from provided X and Y coordinate values, with optional spatial reference system metadata.

### Parameters
- `x` (`double`): The X-coordinate (longitude or easting) of the point.
- `y` (`double`): The Y-coordinate (latitude or northing) of the point.
- `srid` (`crs`): Optional coordinate reference system identifier, provided as an integer (SRID) or a string (e.g., 'EPSG:4326').

### Input
Requires two `double` values for coordinates. An optional third argument can be an integer SRID or a string representing the CRS. No specific file format is required as this is a constructor for in-memory geometry objects.

### Output
Returns a `geometry` object representing a single point in the specified or default coordinate system.

### Valid Call Patterns
```python
# Basic construction
sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128) as geom").to_arrow_table()

# Construction with SRID
sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 4326) as geom").to_arrow_table()

# Construction with CRS string
sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 'EPSG:3857') as geom").to_arrow_table()
```

### LLM Instruction Prompt
- Always use `sd.sql(query).to_arrow_table()` for executing queries.
- When constructing points for spatial predicates, ensure the `srid` matches the reference geometry's CRS to avoid implicit conversion errors.
- Use `ST_AsText()` if you need to verify the geometry output in a human-readable format.

### Prompt Snippet
```text
sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(x_val, y_val, srid_val)").to_arrow_table()
```

### Common Failure Modes
- Providing non-numeric values for `x` or `y` coordinates.
- Mismatched CRS: If performing spatial operations (e.g., `ST_Intersects`) between a point and another geometry, ensure both share the same CRS or are explicitly transformed.
- Invalid CRS strings: Providing a string that does not conform to standard EPSG or OGC formats.

### Fix Code Hint
```python
# Ensure coordinates are floats and CRS is a valid string or integer
x, y = -74.0060, 40.7128
srid = 4326
query = f"SELECT ST_Point({x}, {y}, {srid}) as geom"
result = sd.sql(query).to_arrow_table()
print("__CHECK__")
```

Project context distilled from the original README (grounding — may say none exists):
===== Original documentation part 1/3 =====
### 1. Project purpose
SedonaDB 0.5.0 provides a suite of SQL-based geospatial operations for vector and raster data. It enables users to perform raster-to-vector conversions, raster metadata inspection, grid-based resampling, and spatial analysis (zonal statistics, intersection, containment) within a SQL environment. The project is designed for high-performance geospatial computation, supporting both in-database and out-of-database raster workflows.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Raster Creation & Loading:** Creating empty grids (`RS_MakeEmptyRaster`), loading from file paths (`RS_FromPath`), or decoding binary content (`RS_FromGDALRaster`).
*   **Raster-Geometry Interaction:** Rasterizing geometries (`RS_AsRaster`), clipping rasters to geometries (`RS_Clip`), and computing zonal statistics (`RS_ZonalStats`).
*   **Raster Transformation:** Resampling grids (`RS_Resample`), reprojecting to match a reference (`RS_ReprojectMatch`), and stacking bands (`RS_Stack`).
*   **Metadata & Inspection:** Querying CRS, geotransforms, dimensions, and band statistics (`RS_MetaData`, `RS_SummaryStats`, `RS_DimSize`).
*   **Vectorization:** Converting raster bands to polygons (`RS_Polygonize`) or extracting pixel values at specific points (`RS_Value`).

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Raster Accessors:** `RS_Width`, `RS_Height`, `RS_NumBands`, `RS_CRS`, `RS_SRID`, `RS_GeoReference`.
*   **Band Operations:** `RS_BandIsNoData`, `RS_BandNoDataValue`, `RS_BandPixelType`, `RS_SetBandNoDataValue`.
*   **Dimension Management:** `RS_BandToDim`, `RS_DimToBand`, `RS_Slice`, `RS_SliceRange`.
*   **Spatial Predicates:** `RS_Intersects`, `RS_Contains`, `RS_Within`.
*   **Coordinate Conversion:** `RS_RasterToWorldCoord`, `RS_WorldToRasterCoord`.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Raster Inputs:** Supports GDAL-readable formats (GeoTIFF, PNG, etc.).
*   **Geometry Inputs:** Standard OGC WKT/WKB geometries.
*   **Data Types:** Pixel types include `int8`, `uint8`, `int16`, `uint16`, `int32`, `uint32`, `int64`, `uint64`, `float32`, `float64`.
*   **Configuration:** CRS is represented as strings (e.g., 'EPSG:4326').

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Raster:** In-database raster objects.
*   **Geometry:** Points, Polygons, or Envelopes.
*   **Scalar/Aggregate:** `double`, `bigint`, `boolean`, or `struct` (for metadata/stats).
*   **Binary:** GeoTIFF byte streams via `RS_AsGeoTiff`.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Experimental Status:** Many raster functions (e.g., `RS_Clip`, `RS_Resample`, `RS_ReprojectMatch`) are marked as experimental and subject to change.
*   **CRS Consistency:** Operations like `RS_ReprojectMatch` and `RS_Clip` require consistent CRS handling; errors occur if exactly one input has a CRS.
*   **Memory:** Functions like `RS_ReprojectMatch` and `RS_Resample` (with extent-growing) load the entire raster into memory.

### 7. Commands and examples
**Rasterization:**
```sql
SELECT RS_Width(
  RS_AsRaster(
    RS_ConvexHull(raster),
    raster,
    'float64',
    false,
    1.0,
    0.0,
    false
  )
)
FROM (SELECT RS_FromPath('https://download.osgeo.org/geotiff/samples/gdal_eg/cea.tif') AS raster) r;
```

**Clipping:**
```sql
SELECT RS_Width(
  RS_Clip(
    RS_Example(),
    1,
    ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((60 90, 160 90, 160 190, 60 190, 60 90))', 'OGC:CRS84')
  )
);
```

**Resampling:**
```sql
SELECT RS_Width(RS_Resample(RS_Example(), 32, 16, false, 'NearestNeighbor'));
```

**Zonal Statistics:**
```sql
SELECT RS_ZonalStats(
  RS_Example(),
  ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((60 90, 160 90, 160 190, 60 190, 60 90))', 'OGC:CRS84'),
  1,
  'mean'
);
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
*   **Integer Constraints:** `Int64` and `UInt64` rasters are not supported by `RS_ReprojectMatch` (must cast to `Int32` or `Float64` first).
*   **Band Indexing:** Band indices are 1-based.
*   **Coordinate Systems:** If CRS differs, geometries are reprojected to the raster's CRS. If only one side has a CRS, the operation fails.
*   **Tile Dimensions:** TIFF tile dimensions must be multiples of 16.
*   **Dimension Names:** New dimension names in `RS_BandToDim` must not already exist.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   `RS_AsGeoTiff` returns binary bytes.
*   `RS_MakeEmptyRaster` supports 0 bands for template grids.
*   `RS_Tile` materializes a full copy of the raster in memory.
*   `RS_SummaryStats` and `RS_ZonalStats` support `count`, `sum`, `mean`, `stddev`, `min`, `max`.
*   `RS_Value` and `RS_Values` perform no resampling (nearest-pixel sampling).

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   Specific memory limits for "in-database" rasters are not explicitly defined beyond the 4 GiB per-band limit.
*   The exact behavior of "preferred transformation" fallback to WGS 84 in `RS_Intersects` is not detailed.
*   The "session's geography bounder" mentioned in `RS_MakeEmptyRaster` is not documented in the provided snippets.

===== Original documentation part 2/3 =====
### 1. Project purpose
SedonaDB 0.5.0 provides a suite of SQL-based geospatial operations for vector and raster analysis. It enables users to perform geometric computations, spatial predicates, and aggregations on `geometry` and `geography` types within a SQL environment, accessible via Python using `sedonadb.connect()`.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Data Construction:** Creating geometries/geographies from WKT, WKB, or coordinate primitives (e.g., `ST_Point`, `ST_GeomFromWKT`).
*   **Spatial Analysis:** Computing relationships (e.g., `ST_Contains`, `ST_Intersects`, `ST_DWithin`) and measurements (e.g., `ST_Area`, `ST_Length`, `ST_Distance`).
*   **Geometry Processing:** Modifying or deriving new geometries (e.g., `ST_Buffer`, `ST_Intersection`, `ST_ConvexHull`, `ST_MakeValid`).
*   **Aggregation:** Performing group-wise spatial operations (e.g., `ST_Collect_Agg`, `ST_ConvexHull_Agg`, `ST_Envelope_Agg`).
*   **Coordinate/Dimension Management:** Handling Z/M dimensions (`ST_Force3D`, `ST_Force4D`) and CRS metadata (`ST_CRS`).

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Connection:** `sd = sedonadb.connect()`
*   **Execution:** `sd.sql(query).to_arrow_table()` or `.to_pandas()`.
*   **Views:** Use `.to_view("name")` to register intermediate results for subsequent SQL joins or complex queries.
*   **Naming Conventions:** Most functions follow the OGC standard (e.g., `ST_Area`, `ST_Buffer`). Aliases exist for common functions (e.g., `ST_AsWKT` for `ST_AsText`, `ST_NumInteriorRing` for `ST_NumInteriorRings`).
*   **Aggregate Functions:** Suffix `_Agg` denotes aggregate operations (e.g., `ST_ConvexHull_Agg`).

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Formats:** Well-Known Text (WKT), Well-Known Binary (WKB), Extended WKT (EWKT), and Extended WKB (EWKB).
*   **Types:** `geometry` (planar) and `geography` (spherical).
*   **Primitives:** `double` (coordinates, distances, fractions), `integer` (indices, precision, SRIDs), `string` (WKT, parameters), `binary` (WKB).

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Scalars:** `double`, `integer`, `boolean`, `string`, `binary`.
*   **Geospatial:** `geometry` or `geography` objects.
*   **Structures:** `ST_Dump` returns a `struct` containing expanded geometry parts.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **CRS:** GeoHash operations require WGS84 (`EPSG:4326` / `OGC:CRS84`) or no CRS.
*   **Geography:** Geography operations require the `s2geography` bounder to be registered on the session.
*   **Storage:** `ST_AsBinary` may preserve Arrow `BinaryView` storage; use `sd_simplifystorage()` if standard `Binary` is required.

### 7. Commands and examples
**Basic Geometry Creation:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 4326);
```

**Spatial Predicate:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Contains(
    ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((0 0, 1 0, 1 1, 0 1, 0 0))'),
    ST_Point(0.25, 0.25)
) AS val;
```

**Buffer with Parameters:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Buffer(
  ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((10 10, 11 10, 10 11, 10 10))'),
  1.0,
  'quad_segs=2'
) AS geom;
```

**Aggregate Operation:**
```sql
SELECT ST_ConvexHull_Agg(geom) FROM (
    VALUES
        (ST_Point(0, 0)),
        (ST_Point(0, 1)),
        (ST_Point(1, 0)),
        (ST_Point(1, 1))
) AS t(geom);
```

**Python Integration:**
```python
import sedonadb
sd = sedonadb.connect()
sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(1.0, 2.0) as geom").to_view("input")
df = sd.sql("SELECT ST_AsText(geom) as wkt FROM input").to_pandas()
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
*   **Geography vs. Geometry:** `ST_Area` for geometry returns units of the CRS; for geography, it returns square meters.
*   **Dimension Handling:** `ST_Force3D` and `ST_Force4D` preserve existing Z/M values if present; otherwise, they default to 0.
*   **Validation:** `ST_IsValid` checks OGC compliance; `ST_MakeValid` repairs invalid geometries.
*   **GeoHash:** Precision is capped at 20 characters. Non-positive precision returns an empty string.
*   **Index:** `ST_GeometryN` and `ST_InteriorRingN` use 1-based indexing.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   SedonaDB 0.5.0 supports both planar `geometry` and spherical `geography` types.
*   Aggregate functions are explicitly marked with `_Agg`.
*   The library supports PostGIS-compatible EWKT/EWKB formats.
*   Python interaction is primarily through `sd.sql(...).to_arrow_table()`.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   The documentation for `ST_ReducePrecision` is truncated in the provided source.
*   Specific details on the `sd_simplifystorage()` function are limited to a mention in `ST_AsBinary`.
*   The exact list of supported `params` strings for `ST_Buffer` is referenced as "identical to PostGIS" but not explicitly enumerated.

===== Original documentation part 3/3 =====
### 1. Project purpose
SedonaDB is an open-source, single-node analytical database engine designed for high-performance geospatial computation. It treats spatial data as a first-class citizen, providing comprehensive support for vector and (forthcoming) raster analysis. It is built on Rust, leveraging Apache Arrow and Apache DataFusion for in-memory columnar processing, spatial indexing, and efficient query optimization.

### 2. Main workflows
* **Connection:** Initialize a session using `sedona.db.connect()`.
* **Data Loading:** Load datasets (e.g., Parquet) into a `DataFrame` and register them as SQL views using `df.to_view("name")`.
* **Query Execution:** Execute spatial SQL queries via `sd.sql("...").to_arrow_table()`.
* **Spatial Analysis:** Perform range queries, KNN, spatial joins, and coordinate transformations using the provided `ST_*` SQL function library.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
* **Connection:** `sd = sedona.db.connect()`
* **Execution:** `sd.sql(query).to_arrow_table()`
* **Metadata/CRS:** Functions like `ST_SetCRS`, `ST_SetSRID`, and `ST_Transform` manage spatial reference systems.
* **Geometry Manipulation:** Operations include `ST_ReducePrecision`, `ST_Reverse`, `ST_Rotate`, `ST_Scale`, `ST_Segmentize`, `ST_Simplify`, `ST_Snap`, `ST_Translate`, and `ST_UnaryUnion`.
* **Predicates:** `ST_Relate`, `ST_Touches`, `ST_Within`.
* **Accessors:** `ST_X`, `ST_Y`, `ST_Z`, `ST_XMin`, `ST_XMax`, `ST_YMin`, `ST_YMax`, `ST_ZMin`, `ST_ZMax`, `ST_SRID`, `ST_Zmflag`, `ST_StartPoint`.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
* **Vector:** GeoParquet, WKT, WKB, and formats supported by GeoPandas.
* **Raster:** Support is planned (compatible with SedonaSpark).
* **CRS:** Accepts authority codes (e.g., `'EPSG:4326'`), PROJJSON, or WKT.

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
* Results are returned as PyArrow tables.
* Spatial operations return `geometry` or `geography` types, or scalar values (double, integer, boolean, string) depending on the function.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
* Designed for single-node local or cloud instances.
* Assumes availability of PyArrow-compatible environments.
* Metadata-only operations (e.g., `ST_SetCRS`, `ST_ToGeography`) do not modify underlying coordinate data.

### 7. Commands and examples
**Reduce Precision:**
```sql
SELECT ST_ReducePrecision(
    ST_GeogFromWKT('POINT (1.123456 2.654321)'),
    0.01
);
```

**Relate (DE-9IM):**
```sql
SELECT ST_Relate(
    ST_GeomFromText('POINT (0.5 0.5)'),
    ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((0 0, 1 0, 1 1, 0 1, 0 0))'),
    '0FFFFF212'
);
```

**Simplify:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Simplify(
    ST_GeomFromWKT('LINESTRING (0 0, 0.5 0.1, 1 0, 1.5 0.1, 2 0)'),
    0.2
);
```

**Transform:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Transform(ST_SetCRS(ST_Point(-122.35, 47.65), 'EPSG:4326'), 'EPSG:26910');
```

**Union Aggregate:**
```sql
SELECT ST_Union_Agg(geom) FROM (
  VALUES
    (ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((0 0, 0 2, 2 2, 2 0, 0 0))')),
    (ST_GeomFromText('POLYGON ((1 1, 1 3, 3 3, 3 1, 1 1))'))
) AS t(geom);
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules, and type-selection rules
* **Geography vs. Geometry:** Many functions have separate kernels for `geometry` (planar) and `geography` (spherical).
* **Transformation:** `ST_Transform` requires target CRS to be geographic if the input is `geography`.
* **Validity:** `ST_Simplify` may produce invalid geometries; use `ST_SimplifyPreserveTopology` for validity guarantees.
* **Metadata:** `ST_SetCRS` and `ST_SetSRID` are metadata-only; they do not reproject coordinates.
* **Antimeridian:** `ST_TessellateGeom` handles antimeridian crossing by wrapping coordinates, but fails for polygons spanning the full longitude range (e.g., Antarctica).

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
* SedonaDB is a single-node engine built in Rust.
* It provides dual Python and SQL APIs.
* It maintains CRS propagation throughout operations.
* It is interoperable with the broader PyArrow ecosystem (DuckDB, Polars, GeoPandas).

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
* Specifics on the "Raster" function API are currently missing (marked as "coming soon").
* The exact list of supported EPSG codes or PROJ string validation rules is not explicitly detailed beyond general references to CRS concepts.
* Documentation for `ST_Zmflag` return codes is clear, but the behavior for mixed-dimension collections is not explicitly defined.

# SedonaDB

[![PyPI Version](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/sedonadb)](https://pypi.org/project/sedonadb/) [![PyPI Downloads](https://static.pepy.tech/personalized-badge/sedonadb?period=monthly&units=NONE&left_color=BLACK&right_color=GREEN&left_text=downloads%2Fmonth)](https://pepy.tech/projects/sedonadb) [![Downloads](https://static.pepy.tech/personalized-badge/sedonadb?period=total&units=international_system&left_color=black&right_color=brightgreen&left_text=total%20downloads)](https://pepy.tech/project/sedonadb)
[![Anaconda Version](
https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb/badges/version.svg)](https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb) [![Conda Downloads](https://anaconda.org/conda-forge/sedonadb/badges/downloads.svg)](https://anaconda.org/channels/conda-forge/packages/sedonadb/overview)
[![R-multiverse
status](https://img.shields.io/badge/dynamic/json?url=https%3A%2F%2Fcommunity.r-multiverse.org%2Fapi%2Fpackages%2Fsedonadb&query=%24.Version&label=r-multiverse)](https://community.r-multiverse.org/sedonadb)

SedonaDB is an open-source single-node analytical database engine with **geospatial as a first-class citizen**. It aims to deliver the fastest spatial analytics query speed and the most comprehensive function coverage available.

SedonaDB is perfect for processing smaller to medium datasets on local machines or cloud instances. For distributed workloads, you can leverage the power of SedonaSpark, SedonaFlink, or SedonaSnow.

## Architecture

![SedonaDB Architecture](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sedonadb-architecture.svg)

* **Columnar in-memory datasets**
    * Spatial indexing
    * Spatial statistics
    * CRS tracking
    * Arrow format and zero serialization overhead

* **Spatial query optimization**
    * Spatial-aware heuristic based optimization
    * Spatial-aware cost based optimization
    * Automatic disk spilling for large-scale spatial joins

* **Spatial query processing**
    * Spatial range query, KNN query, spatial join query, KNN join query
    * Map algebra, NDVI, mask, zonal statistics

Raster functions are coming soon. We expect SedonaDB Raster will match all raster functions provided in [SedonaSpark](https://sedona.apache.org/latest/api/sql/Raster-operators/).

## Features of SedonaDB

SedonaDB has several advantages:

* **High Performance:** Built in Rust for exceptional speed and memory efficiency
* **Comprehensive Spatial Toolkit:** Supports both vector and raster functions in a single library
* **CRS Propagation:** Always maintains coordinate reference system information
* **Format Flexibility:** Supports legacy and modern file formats including GeoParquet, Shapefile, GeoJSON
* **Dual APIs:** Python and SQL interfaces for seamless workflow integration
* **Extensible:** Easily customizable and extensible architecture
* **Ecosystem Integration:** Interoperable with PyArrow-compatible libraries like GeoPandas, DuckDB, and Polars
* **Active Community:** Great maintainers and contributors who encourage external contributions

## Performance Benchmarks

This is a performance benchmark comparing SedonaDB 0.1.0, DuckDB 1.4.0, and GeoPandas 1.1.1 using SpatialBench Queries 1-12 at Scale Factors 1 and 10. Details can be found at [Apache Sedona SpatialBench](https://sedona.apache.org/spatialbench/single-node-benchmarks/).

<div align="center">
  <img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sf1-09242025.png" alt="SF1 Benchmark Results" width="45%" />
  <img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/sedona-db/refs/heads/main/docs/image/sf10-09242025.png" alt="SF10 Benchmark Results" width="45%" />
</div>

## Install

You can install Python SedonaDB with [PyPI](https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/tutorials/installing-packages/):

```sh
pip install "apache-sedona[db]"
```

Or with [conda](https://www.anaconda.com/docs/getting-started/main):

```sh
conda install sedonadb
```

## Quick Start

Get started with SedonaDB in just a few lines:

```python
import sedona.db

# Connect to SedonaDB
sd = sedona.db.connect()

# Run a simple spatial query
result = sd.sql("SELECT ST_Point(0, 1) as geom")
result.show()
```

### Supported File Formats

SedonaDB supports a wide range of geospatial file formats:

- **Vector:** GeoParquet, WKT, WKB, all formats supported by GeoPandas
- **Raster:** Coming soon with full SedonaSpark compatibility

## Overture buildings example

This section shows how to query the Overture buildings data.

Start by establishing a connection:

```python
import sedona.db

sd = sedona.db.connect()
```

Read the dataset into a Python SedonaDB `DataFrame`. This is lazy: even though the Overture buildings table contains millions of rows, SedonaDB will only fetch the data required for the query.

```python
df = sd.read_parquet(
    "s3://overturemaps-us-west-2/release/2026-02-18.0/theme=buildings/type=building/",
    options={"aws.skip_signature": True, "aws.region": "us-west-2"},
)
df.to_view("buildings")
```

Now run a query to compute the centroids of tall buildings (above 20 meters) in New York City:

```python
nyc_bbox_wkt = (
    "POLYGON((-74.2591 40.4774, -74.2591 40.9176, -73.7004 40.9176, -73.7004 40.4774, -74.2591 40.4774))"
)

sd.sql(f"""
SELECT
    id,
    height,
    num_floors,
    roof_shape,
    ST_Centroid(geometry) as centroid
FROM
    buildings
WHERE
    is_underground = FALSE
    AND height IS NOT NULL
    AND height > 20
    AND ST_Intersects(geometry, ST_GeomFromText('{nyc_bbox_wkt}', 4326))
LIMIT 5;
""").show()
```

title: ST_Point
description: Constructs a Point Geometry from X and Y.
kernels:
  - returns: geometry
    args:

      type: double
    - name: "y"
      type: double
    - name: srid
      type: crs

## Description

Constructs a Point geometry from X and Y coordinates. An optional SRID or CRS can be provided as a third argument.

## Examples

```sql
SELECT ST_AsText(ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128));
```

With an SRID:

```sql
SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 4326);
```

With a CRS:

```sql
SELECT ST_Point(-74.0060, 40.7128, 'EPSG:3857');
```

===== Shared original document: README.md =====
# ![Beast](doc/images/beast-logo.svg)
Beast is a system for Big Exploratory Analytics on Spatio-Temporal data. It supports both vector and raster data with
multidimensional data types and index structures. Here is a short list of the most important components of Beast.

![Beast Splash Screen](doc/images/splash.svg)

- **Multidimensional data**: Beast can work with arbitrarily multidimensional data.
- **Input/Output formats**: Beast supports many standard formats including Esri Shapefile, CSV, JSON+WKT, GeoJSON, KML, KMZ, and GPX.
- **Scalable visualization**: Visualize terabytes of data on an interactive map. Beast powers [UCR-Star](https://star.cs.ucr.edu) the home of terabytes of public geospatial data.
- **Raptor = Raster + Vector**: Beast hosts a novel component for processing raster and vector data concurrently. It is orders of magnitude faster than competitive systems.
- **Data Generator**: [Spider](https://spider.cs.ucr.edu) is an award-winning scalable data generator for benchmarking and testing.

## Documentation
- [Getting Started](doc/Home.md)
- [Installation](doc/installation.md)
- [Development Setup](doc/dev-setup.md)
- [Input/Output Formats](doc/input-output.md)
- [UCR-Star](https://star.cs.ucr.edu) powered by Beast
- [Spider Web](https://spider.cs.ucr.edu)
- [SatEx Satellite Data Extractor](satex/README.md)

## Questions

If you have any questions about any of the packages or modules of beast,
please ask your question on [StackOverflow](https://stackoverflow.com/)
and tag your questions with `#ucrbeast` and `#beast`. Beast team members
follow these tags and will respond to those questions as soon as possible.

## Issues

If you found any bugs or have any recommended features to add, please
[open a ticket](https://bitbucket.org/bdlabucr/beast/issues/new)
on the project homepage.

## API Test: `java:edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.raptor.Statistics.isValid`

### Signature
```java
():boolean
```
_Source: source/raptor/src/main/java/edu/ucr/cs/bdlab/raptor/Statistics.java:131_

### Goal
Checks if the current `Statistics` object contains valid, computed statistical data ready for retrieval or further processing.

### Parameters
_None._

### Input
The caller must have an initialized `edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.raptor.Statistics` object. This method is typically called after a raster-vector join or a zonal statistics operation has been performed on the object to ensure the computation completed successfully.

### Output
Returns `boolean` — `true` if the statistics have been successfully calculated and are valid; `false` otherwise.

### Valid Call Patterns
```java
// Inferred from signature:
edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.raptor.Statistics stats = new edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.raptor.Statistics();
// ... perform operations ...
if (stats.isValid()) {
    // Access statistical results
}
```

### LLM Instruction Prompt
- When generating code that accesses statistical results from an `edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.raptor.Statistics` instance, always wrap the access in a conditional check using `isValid()` to prevent processing uninitialized or invalid data.

### Prompt Snippet
```text
if (statisticsInstance.isValid()) {
    // Proceed with data extraction
}
```

### Common Failure Modes
- **Uninitialized State:** Calling `isValid()` on a `Statistics` object that has not yet been populated by a Raptor operation will return `false`.
- **Post-Processing Errors:** If a raster-vector join fails due to CRS mismatch or incompatible data types, the associated `Statistics` object may remain in an invalid state. Always verify `isValid()` before attempting to read values from the object.

### Fix Code Hint
```java
// Ensure the statistics object is valid before usage
if (!stats.isValid()) {
    throw new IllegalStateException("Statistics computation failed or was not performed.");
}
```

Project context distilled from the original README (grounding — may say none exists):
===== Original documentation part 1/1 =====
### 1. Project purpose
Beast is a Spark-based system for Big Exploratory Analytics on Spatio-Temporal data. It provides a unified framework for processing and visualizing both vector (Shapefile, GeoJSON, etc.) and raster (GeoTIFF, HDF) data. Its core component, **Raptor** (Raster-Plus-Vector), enables high-performance concurrent processing of raster and vector datasets.

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Spatial Indexing:** Building distributed spatial indexes (e.g., R*-Grove) for efficient querying and visualization.
*   **Spatial Joins:** Performing distributed joins between vector-vector or raster-vector datasets using predicates like `Intersects`, `Contains`, and `MBRIntersects`.
*   **Raster Processing (RDPro):** Performing pixel-level operations (map, filter, overlay, reshape) on satellite data.
*   **Visualization:** Generating single-image plots or multilevel interactive pyramid tiles (MVT/PNG) for web exploration.
*   **Data Generation:** Creating synthetic spatial datasets (points, boxes, polygons) for benchmarking.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Contexts:** Use `JavaSpatialSparkContext` (Java) or `import edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.beast._` (Scala) to access spatial RDD extensions.
*   **Raster/Vector Join:** `raptorJoin` combines raster tiles with vector features.
*   **Spatial Partitioning:** `spatialPartition(classOf[RSGrovePartitioner])` is the recommended method for load balancing.
*   **Web Integration:** Extend `AbstractWebHandler` and annotate methods with `@WebMethod` to add custom REST endpoints to the Beast server.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Vector:** Esri Shapefile, GeoJSON, JSON+WKT, GPX, CSV (delimited text).
*   **Raster:** GeoTIFF, HDF4.
*   **Auto-detection:** Beast can auto-detect formats based on file extensions or content analysis (for CSV).

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Spatial Files:** Saved via `saveAsShapefile`, `saveAsGeoJSON`, `saveAsKML`, or `writeSpatialFile`.
*   **Visualization:** PNG images, MVT (Mapbox Vector Tiles) pyramids, and interactive HTML/JS interfaces.
*   **Summaries:** JSON-formatted metadata including MBR, feature counts, and geometry types.

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Environment:** Requires JDK 1.8+, Apache Maven/SBT, and a configured Spark cluster.
*   **CLI:** The `beast` command-line tool is the primary entry point for prepackaged operations.
*   **Server:** The `beast server` command starts a Jetty-based visualization and API server on port 8890.

### 7. Commands and examples
**Indexing:**
```shell
beast index tl_2018_us_primaryroads.zip iformat:shapefile tl_2018_us_primaryroads_index gindex:rsgrove oformat:rtree
```

**Multilevel Plotting:**
```shell
beast mplot tl_2018_us_primaryroads_index iformat:rtree tl_2018_us_primaryroads_plot.zip levels:20 plotter:gplot -mercator
```

**Raptor Join (Scala):**
```scala
val join: RDD[RaptorJoinFeature[Float]] = treecover.raptorJoin(countries)
  .filter(v => v.m >= 0 && v.m <= 100.0)
```

**Spatial Join (Java):**
```java
JavaPairRDD<IFeature, IFeature> sjResults = JavaSpatialRDDHelper.spatialJoin(matchedPolygons, matchedPoints,
    SpatialJoinAlgorithms.ESJPredicate.Contains, SpatialJoinAlgorithms.ESJDistributedAlgorithm.PBSM);
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules
*   **Output Directories:** Operations fail if the output directory exists; use `-overwrite` to force deletion.
*   **Raptor Join:** Raster and vector data must be compatible (e.g., same CRS). Use `reproject` or `reshape` to align them first.
*   **Partitioning:** `spatialJoin` performs best when both datasets are spatially partitioned.
*   **Type Selection:** When loading GeoTIFFs, the type parameter (e.g., `[Int]`, `[Float]`) must match the file's pixel type.

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   Beast is a Spark add-on; it does not replace Spark but extends it with spatial RDDs.
*   The `R*-Grove` partitioner is the recommended choice for balanced spatial partitioning.
*   The `beast server` provides a REST API for dataset management and visualization.
*   The project uses `beast-spark` as the primary Maven dependency.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   Specific API documentation for `RaptorJoinFeature` methods is sparse.
*   The distinction between `reshapeNN` and `reshapeAverage` is documented, but performance benchmarks for different interpolation methods are not provided.
*   The `beast.xml` configuration schema for custom web handlers is not fully detailed.
*   Not clearly documented: The exact behavior of `raptorJoin` when the raster and vector data have different coordinate systems without explicit reprojection.

## API Test: `scala:edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.beast.geolite.RasterMetadataSerializer.isImmutable`

### Signature
```scala
[]:Boolean
```
_Source: source/cg/src/main/scala/edu/ucr/cs/bdlab/beast/geolite/RasterMetadataSerializer.scala:53_

### Goal
Determines whether the current `RasterMetadataSerializer` instance is configured as immutable, indicating that its metadata state cannot be modified after initialization.

### Parameters
_None._

### Input
No input parameters are required. The caller must ensure that the `RasterMetadataSerializer` instance has been properly instantiated within the context of a raster processing workflow.

### Output
Returns `Boolean` — `true` if the serializer is immutable, `false` otherwise.

### Valid Call Patterns
```scala
// Inferred from signature:
val serializer = new edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.beast.geolite.RasterMetadataSerializer()
val isReadOnly = serializer.isImmutable
```

### LLM Instruction Prompt
- When generating code involving `RasterMetadataSerializer`, use `isImmutable` to check the mutability state of the serializer before attempting any configuration changes.
- Do not pass any arguments to this method as it takes zero parameters.

### Prompt Snippet
```text
val serializer = new edu.ucr.cs.bdlab.beast.geolite.RasterMetadataSerializer()
if (!serializer.isImmutable) {
  // Perform configuration
}
```

### Common Failure Modes
- **Null Pointer Exception:** Calling this method on an uninitialized or null `RasterMetadataSerializer` reference.
- **Assumption of Mutability:** Attempting to modify metadata on a serializer instance where `isImmutable` returns `true`, which may lead to runtime exceptions depending on the specific implementation of the serializer.

### Fix Code Hint
```scala
// Ensure the serializer is mutable before applying changes
if (!serializer.isImmutable) {
  // Apply metadata updates here
} else {
  throw new IllegalStateException("Cannot modify immutable RasterMetadataSerializer")
}
```

Project context distilled from the original README (grounding — may say none exists):
===== Original documentation part 1/1 =====
This distillation provides the factual foundation for generating documentation for the Beast (v0.10.1) geospatial framework.

### 1. Project purpose
Beast is a Spark-based system for Big Exploratory Analytics on Spatio-Temporal data. It provides a unified framework for processing vector and raster data, supporting multidimensional indexing, scalable visualization, and concurrent raster-vector processing (Raptor).

### 2. Main workflows
*   **Spatial Indexing:** Building distributed spatial indexes (e.g., R*-Grove) for efficient querying and join operations.
*   **Spatial Joins:** Performing joins between vector-vector or raster-vector datasets using distributed algorithms (BNLJ, PBSM, DJ, REPJ).
*   **Raster Processing (RDPro):** Pixel-level transformations, filtering, and reshaping (reprojection, rescaling) of raster data.
*   **Visualization:** Generating single-image plots or multilevel pyramid tiles (MVT/PNG) for interactive web exploration.
*   **Data Generation:** Creating synthetic spatial datasets (points, boxes, polygons) for benchmarking.

### 3. Important APIs and usage patterns
*   **Entry Points:** `SparkContext` (Scala) or `JavaSpatialSparkContext` (Java) are extended with spatial methods.
*   **RDD Types:** `RDD[IFeature]` for vector data; `RasterRDD[T]` or `RDD[ITile[T]]` for raster data.
*   **Spatial Partitioning:** `features.spatialPartition(classOf[RSGrovePartitioner])` is the recommended approach for load balancing.
*   **Raptor Join:** `raster.raptorJoin[T](vector)` combines raster pixels with vector geometries.
*   **Web Integration:** `AbstractWebHandler` allows custom web operations via `@WebMethod` annotations.

### 4. Inputs and file formats
*   **Vector:** Shapefile (ZIP or directory), GeoJSON, JSON+WKT, GPX, CSV (delimited points/WKT/envelopes).
*   **Raster:** GeoTIFF, HDF4.
*   **Auto-detection:** Beast attempts to detect formats based on file extensions or content analysis.

### 5. Outputs and generated artifacts
*   **Spatial Files:** Saved via `saveAsShapefile`, `saveAsGeoJSON`, `saveAsKML`, `saveAsWKTFile`, or `writeSpatialFile`.
*   **Visualization:** PNG images (`splot`), multilevel pyramid tiles (`mplot`, `vmplot`), and MVT vector tiles.
*   **Summaries:** `Summary` objects (MBR, count, size, geometry type) and histograms (count/size, simple/Euler).

### 6. Configuration and environment assumptions
*   **Dependencies:** JDK 1.8+, Apache Maven/SBT, Spark 3.x (implied).
*   **CLI:** Requires `beast` binary in `PATH`.
*   **Development:** Add `beast-spark` dependency to `pom.xml`.
*   **Server:** `beast server` starts a Jetty-based web UI on port 8890.

### 7. Commands and examples
**Spatial Indexing:**
```shell
beast index tl_2018_us_primaryroads.zip iformat:shapefile tl_2018_us_primaryroads_index gindex:rsgrove oformat:rtree
```

**Multilevel Plotting:**
```shell
beast mplot tl_2018_us_primaryroads_index iformat:rtree tl_2018_us_primaryroads_plot.zip levels:20 plotter:gplot -mercator
```

**Raptor Join (Scala):**
```scala
val join: RDD[RaptorJoinFeature[Float]] = treecover.raptorJoin(countries)
  .filter(v => v.m >= 0 && v.m <= 100.0)
```

**Raster Processing (Scala):**
```scala
val temperatureF: RasterRDD[Float] = temperatureK.mapPixels(k => (k-273.15f) * 9 / 5 + 32)
temperatureF.saveAsGeoTiff("temperature_f")
```

**Spatial Data Generation (Scala):**
```scala
val randomPoints: RDD[IFeature] = sc.generateSpatialData.uniform(1000000000)
```

### 8. Constraints, preconditions, compatibility rules
*   **Output Directories:** Operations fail if the output directory exists; use `-overwrite` to force deletion.
*   **Raster Compatibility:** `overlay` operations require identical metadata (resolution, CRS, tile size). Use `reshape` to align rasters first.
*   **Partitioning:** `spatialJoin` performance is optimized when both datasets are spatially partitioned.
*   **Type Selection:** `sc.geoTiff[T]` must match the pixel type (IntegerType, FloatType, Array[Int], Array[Float]).

### 9. Facts to preserve in the final README
*   Beast is a Spark add-on; it does not replace Spark but extends it with spatial RDDs.
*   The `RSGrovePartitioner` is the recommended partitioner for most spatial workloads.
*   The `beast-shell` provides an interactive Scala environment with Beast classes pre-linked.
*   The `Data Explorer` module exposes local files via REST API; use with caution in public environments.

### 10. Missing or weak documentation
*   Specific Spark version compatibility (e.g., Spark 3.2 vs 3.5) is not explicitly stated.
*   Detailed API documentation for `RaptorJoinFeature` fields (e.g., `v.m`, `v.feature`) is sparse.
*   The distinction between `mplot` (raster-based) and `vmplot` (vector-tile-based) could be clearer for users choosing a visualization strategy.
*   HDF4 support details are limited compared to GeoTIFF.
